Scope and question
Respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) is one of the leading causes of lower respiratory tract infections and hospitalization during the first year of life.
Evidence base and method
A non-systematic review of the scientific literature was conducted, focusing on real-world effectiveness (RWE) studies carried out in Italy following the introduction of Nirsevimab during the 2024/2025 season.
Consensus and conclusions
Real-world effectiveness data available in the scientific literature from different Italian regions have confirmed the high effectiveness of Nirsevimab observed in clinical trials, in line with findings reported in the international literature. Studies conducted in Italy have reported immunization effectiveness in real-world settings exceeding 85% in preventing severe RSV disease, over 80% in preventing RSV-related hospitalizations in neonatal intensive care units (NICUs) or pediatric wards, and over 70% in preventing emergency department visits for RSV-related illness.
Controversies and gaps
The effectiveness of Nirsevimab can translate into a meaningful public health benefit only if high immunization coverage is achieved, ensuring protection for both in-season and out-of-season births, the latter currently representing a key challenge, with lower uptake rates. It is also important to document informed parental refusal among those who decline prophylaxis and to ensure re-immunization during the second RSV season for vulnerable children (i.e., the former palivizumab-eligible cohort).
Research and practice implications
Furthermore, RespivirNet surveillance data should guide the definition of the immunization window on an annual basis, including consideration of a possible extension of immunization at birth through April 30, in view of the progressively later onset of RSV circulation at both the community and hospital levels, particularly in Southern Italian regions. In summary, nirsevimab represents not merely a new preventive strategy, but a paradigm shift: a true revolution in RSV prevention.

01 | 在“革命”后婴儿呼吸道合胞病毒疾病的综合预防:西西里和卡拉布里亚前两个季节的反思

01 | Integrated prevention of respiratory syncytial virus in infants after the “revolution”: reflections from the first two seasons in Sicily and Calabria
作者： E. Amodio, M. Maranto, G. Serra
摘要中文翻译母体免疫接种和单克隆抗体预防措施的引入已显著改变了婴儿呼吸道合胞病毒(呼吸道合胞病毒RSV)疾病的预防方式。在实施了两个季节后,西西里和卡拉布里亚的经验为讨论这些策略的临床影响以及其在实施过程中所涉及的组织挑战提供了机会。材料与方法。本叙述性贡献基于与两位参与RSV预防的医疗专业人员进行的结构化讨论。第一视角关注新策略对儿科的影响,包括住院人数的变化、疾病严重程度以及仍需住院治疗的婴儿特征。第二视角聚焦于母体免疫接种,特别涉及孕妇的接受度、沟通需求以及将呼吸道合胞病毒纳入分娩路径的整合情况。前两个季节表明,严重RSV疾病的负担显著减少。在西西里,2024–2025赛季中,超过65%的目标人群接受了预防保护,RSV 呼吸道合胞病毒相关的住院人数减少了约40%。然而,仍有残留住院病例,尤其是在未及时接受预防措施的婴儿、非常年幼的婴儿以及有潜在脆弱性的儿童中。从母体免疫接种的角度来看,主要挑战涉及及时识别符合条件的孕妇、产科单位与疫苗接种服务之间的协调,以及医疗专业人员提供的信息一致性。接受度可能受到组织障碍以及沟通和文化因素的影响。前两个季节的经验表明,RSV预防的效果不仅取决于母体免疫接种和单克隆抗体预防措施的可获得性,还取决于这些干预措施能否整合到常规护理路径中。加强产科单位、疫苗接种服务、儿科医生和社区医疗专业人员之间的合作将是提高及时获取服务、减少残留可预防疾病以及确保西西里和卡拉布里亚婴儿公平保护的关键。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
本综述聚焦于婴儿呼吸道合胞病毒(RSV)疾病的预防,特别是在引入母体免疫接种和单克隆抗体预防措施之后。核心问题是这些新策略如何影响西西里和卡拉布里亚的临床结果和组织挑战。
证据基础与方法
证据基础来源于基于结构化讨论的叙述性综述,讨论对象为两位参与RSV预防的医疗专业人员。综述未报告具体数据库、检索日期或元分析方法,但包含了对研究设计和人群的一般描述。
共识与结论
前两个季节的实施表明,严重RSV疾病的负担显著减少。RSV预防策略的有效性不仅取决于母体免疫接种和单克隆抗体预防措施的可获得性,还取决于这些干预措施能否整合到常规护理路径中。
争议与缺口
残留住院病例仍持续发生,尤其是在未及时接受预防措施的婴儿、非常年幼的婴儿以及有潜在脆弱性的儿童中。综述强调了在及时识别符合条件的孕妇、产科单位与疫苗接种服务之间的协调以及医疗专业人员提供信息一致性方面的挑战。
科研与实践启示
加强产科单位、疫苗接种服务、儿科医生和社区医疗专业人员之间的合作将是提高及时获取服务、减少残留可预防疾病以及确保西西里和卡拉布里亚婴儿公平保护的关键。

01 | Integrated prevention of respiratory syncytial virus in infants after the “revolution”: reflections from the first two seasons in Sicily and Calabria

Authors: E. Amodio, M. Maranto, G. Serra
Original abstract (English)
The introduction of maternal immunization and monoclonal antibody prophylaxis has substantially changed the prevention of respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) disease in infants. After two seasons of implementation, the experience of Sicily and Calabria offers an opportunity to discuss the clinical impact of these strategies and the organisational challenges involved in their delivery. Materials and Methods. This narrative contribution is based on a structured discussion with two healthcare professionals involved in RSV prevention. The first perspective concerns the paediatric impact of the new strategies, including changes in hospital admissions, disease severity and the characteristics of infants who continue to require hospital care. The second focuses on maternal immunization, with particular reference to acceptance among pregnant women, communication needs and the integration of vaccination into birth pathways. The first two seasons suggest a substantial reduction in the burden of severe RSV disease. In Sicily, during the 2024–2025 season, more than 65% of the target population received preventive protection and hospitalizations for RSV bronchiolitis decreased by approximately 40%. However, residual hospitalizations continue to occur, particularly among infants who did not receive timely prophylaxis, very young infants and children with underlying vulnerability. From the perspective of maternal immunization, the main challenges concern the timely identification of eligible women, the coordination between maternity units and vaccination services, and the consistency of information provided by healthcare professionals. Acceptance may be influenced by organisational barriers as well as by communication and cultural factors. The experience of the first two seasons indicates that the effectiveness of RSV prevention depends not only on the availability of maternal immunization and monoclonal antibody prophylaxis, but also on the ability to integrate these interventions into routine care pathways. Strengthening collaboration among maternity units, vaccination services, paediatricians and community healthcare professionals will be essential to improve timely access, reduce residual preventable disease and ensure equitable protection for infants in Sicily and Calabria.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review focuses on the prevention of respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) disease in infants, particularly after the introduction of maternal immunization and monoclonal antibody prophylaxis. The central question is how these new strategies have impacted the clinical outcomes and organizational challenges in Sicily and Calabria.
Evidence base and method
The evidence base is derived from a narrative review based on a structured discussion with two healthcare professionals involved in RSV prevention. The review does not report on specific databases, search dates, or meta-analysis methods, but it includes general descriptions of the study design and population.
Consensus and conclusions
The first two seasons of implementation suggest a substantial reduction in the burden of severe RSV disease. The effectiveness of RSV prevention strategies depends not only on the availability of maternal immunization and monoclonal antibody prophylaxis but also on the integration of these interventions into routine care pathways.
Controversies and gaps
Residual hospitalizations continue to occur, particularly among infants who did not receive timely prophylaxis, very young infants, and children with underlying vulnerabilities. The review highlights challenges in the timely identification of eligible women, coordination between maternity units and vaccination services, and the consistency of information provided by healthcare professionals.
Research and practice implications
Strengthening collaboration among maternity units, vaccination services, paediatricians, and community healthcare professionals will be essential to improve timely access, reduce residual preventable disease, and ensure equitable protection for infants in Sicily and Calabria.

Respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) maternal vaccination coverage in England: June 2026 report - GOV.UK
新闻简报英国卫生安全局2026年6月的报告提供了英格兰自2024年9月启动的产妇RSV 疫苗接种计划的最新进展。报告指出,2026年6月分娩的女性中,有62.0%在孕期接受了RSV 疫苗。该接种率在NHS委托地区间存在显著差异,西南地区报告最高接种率为71.9%,而伦敦最低为54.6%。此外,接种率在整合照护委员会(ICB)间也有所不同,从多塞特的76.0%到伯明翰和索利赫尔的39.5%。种族差异也有所体现,亚洲或亚洲英国-中国群体的接种率最高,为75.0%,而黑人-加勒比群体最低,为35.4%。数据基于自2025年1月以来超过97.5%的全科医生(GP)诊所的信息,反映了数据质量的提升以及对计划覆盖范围更具代表性的分析。该报告作为官方统计数据发布,旨在监测全国疫苗接种计划对降低RSV相关疾病、住院和死亡负担的影响,尤其是对6个月以下婴儿和老年人群的影响。

该报告于2026年11月5日发布,数据反映了2026年6月分娩女性的疫苗接种率,即自2024年9月计划启动以来的22个月后的数据。

该报告聚焦于英格兰,具体涉及英格兰的NHS委托地区和整合照护委员会(ICBs),数据按种族群体进行细分。

该报告详细说明了英格兰的产妇RSV 疫苗接种接种覆盖率,包括按NHS委托地区、整合照护委员会(ICB)和种族的接种率,作为2024年9月启动的全国疫苗接种计划的一部分。
规模、影响与风险
2026年6月RSV产妇疫苗接种率为62.0%,地区差异最高达17.3个百分点,种族差异最高达39.6个百分点。该计划旨在减少RSV相关疾病、住院和死亡的负担,特别是对6个月以下婴儿和老年人群。
应对、状态与不确定性

A high priorityPublic-health news

Respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) maternal vaccination coverage in England: June 2026 report - GOV.UK

News Brief
The June 2026 report from the UK Health Security Agency provides an update on the maternal RSV vaccination programme in England, which began in September 2024. The report highlights that 62.0% of women who delivered in June 2026 received the RSV vaccine during pregnancy. This uptake varied significantly by NHS commissioning region, with the South West region reporting the highest uptake at 71.9% and London the lowest at 54.6%. Additionally, uptake differed by integrated care board (ICB), ranging from 76.0% in Dorset to 39.5% in Birmingham and Solihull. Ethnic disparities were also noted, with the Asian or Asian British - Chinese group having the highest uptake at 75.0% and the Black - Caribbean group the lowest at 35.4%. The data is based on information from over 97.5% of GP practices since January 2025, reflecting improved data quality and a more representative analysis of the programme's reach. The report serves as an official statistics release and aims to monitor the impact of the national immunisation programme in reducing the burden of RSV-related illness, hospitalisations, and deaths, especially among infants under six months and older adults.
Original source text (English)

It includes vaccine uptake estimates for pregnant women who delivered in June 2026 following the introduction of the RSV maternal vaccination programme in September 2024. Main points The main messages of this report are: 62.0% (26,252 out of 42,326) of women who reported giving birth during the reporting month received an RSV vaccine uptake varied by NHS commissioning region, with the highest uptake reported in the South West region (71.9%) and the lowest in the London region (54.6%) uptake varied across integrated care boards (ICBs), with the highest uptake reported in the Dorset ICB (76.0%) and the lowest in Birmingham and Solihull ICB (39.5%) uptake varied across local authorities, with the highest uptake reported in West Berkshire (86.6%) and the lowest in Enfield (34.5%) uptake varied by ethnic group, with the highest uptake reported among the Asian or Asian British - Chinese ethnic group (75.0%), and the lowest among the Black - Caribbean ethnic group (35.4%) Help us improve this resource This vaccine uptake report has been designated as an official statistics release. Analysis of maternal RSV vaccine uptake Maternal uptake of the RSV vaccine is assessed monthly to monitor the reach and impact of the national immunisation programme. Given the programme’s design, uptake is defined as the proportion of pregnant women who delivered in the reporting month and had received the RSV vaccine during pregnancy. This metric serves as a proxy for vaccine coverage by infant birth month, reflecting the extent of passive immunity conferred to newborns. This monthly report presents RSV vaccine uptake for women who delivered in June 2026, 22 months after the start of the RSV vaccination programme. The monthly uptake trend among pregnant women who have delivered since the programme’s launch is presented in Figure 1. Overall, the monthly RSV maternal vaccine uptake for June 2026 was 62.0%. RSV uptake increased in the first few months of the programme and has remained steady since October 2025. Figure 1. RSV vaccine uptake (%) trend among pregnant women by month of delivery, September 2024 to June 2026 Note 1: the vertical lines represent a change in the percentage of GP (general practitioner) practices used to calculate RSV vaccine uptake. Up to December 2024, vaccine uptake was estimated based on data from a single supplier covering fewer than 40.5% of GP practices. From January 2025 onwards, vaccine uptake was based on data from more than 97.5% of GP practices and is more representative of national vaccination uptake. This month’s report includes data from both major GP IT suppliers, substantially expanding the data available in the earliest reports (September to December 2024). It includes data from 98.6% of GPs in England, up from 40.4% of participating GPs during the first 4 months (September to December 2024) of the vaccination programme. This has been made possible by improvements in data quality and permits a more representative analysis of uptake and trends across England. To further explore variation in uptake, Table 1 presents uptake by commissioning region, while Table 2 provides the breakdown by ethnicity. As shown in Table 1, there are notable regional differences in uptake, with variation of up to 17.3 percentage points between NHS commissioning regions. Table 1. This includes people whose ethnicity was not recorded, not stated, or who refused to give their ethnicity. The ‘Any other ethnicity code’ category includes individuals whose ethnicity has been recorded using non-standard or legacy codes (that is, codes not aligned with the 2001 or 2011 Census classifications). Uptake varied across ICBs by 36.5 percentage points with the highest uptake reported in Dorset (76.0) and the lowest uptake reported in Birmingham and Solihull (39.5). Figure 2. RSV vaccine uptake (%) among pregnant women delivering in June 2026, by ICB Across the local authorities, uptake differed by 52.1 percentage points with the highest uptake reported in West Berkshire (86.6%) and the lowest uptake reported in Enfield (34.5%). The variation across the local authorities is presented in the map shown in Figure 3. Figure 3. RSV vaccine uptake (%) among pregnant women delivering in June 2026, by local authority Further detail, including uptake figures broken down by local authority and ICB, can be found in the June 2026 supplementary data tables. Since the RSV maternal vaccination programme has now been running for 22 months (up to the reporting month), an increasing number of pregnant women are receiving the vaccine during their eligible window. This growing awareness and notification from gestation week 20 onwards have contributed to a gradual increase in monthly vaccine uptake from 40.5% in September 2024 to the current figure reported for June 2026. As the programme continues and more women are informed earlier in their pregnancies, monthly vaccination uptake is expected to gradually increase. Data sources and methodology Further details on the data sources, methodology, data quality, and limitations are available in the quality and methodology information (QMI) report. The maternal RSV vaccine coverage estimates are calculated using monthly GP practice data automatically submitted to ImmForm by participating GP IT suppliers and validated and analysed by the UK Health Security Agency (UKHSA). The report covers women who delivered during the reporting month (1 June 2026 to 30 June 2026), excluding miscarriages and stillbirths, with uptake measured as receipt of RSV vaccination from 28 weeks of pregnancy up to delivery. To ensure complete and accurate recording of vaccination and delivery information, data are extracted using a three-month reporting lag. The June 2026 estimates are based on data from the two major GP IT suppliers, representing 98.6% of participating GP practices nationally. The July 2026 maternal RSV vaccination uptake report is scheduled to be released on 3 December 2026. Background information The RSV vaccine has been offered to pregnant women in England since September 2024 to address the significant burden of RSV-related illness, hospitalisations and deaths, particularly among infants under six months of age and older adults, who are at increased risk of lower respiratory tract infection (LRTI) (see the Green Book on immunisation). In 2023, the Joint Committee on Vaccination and Immunisation (JCVI) issued a full statement recommending the implementation of a maternal RSV vaccination programme. This recommendation was based on robust evidence demonstrating the safety and effectiveness of RSV vaccines in protecting infants during their most vulnerable period. The vaccine is recommended as a year-round programme to be administered from 28 weeks of gestation onwards. This timing aligns with the optimal window for passive antibody transfer to the foetus, ensuring effective protection against severe RSV-related illness. From 1 September 2024 (the programme start date), all women who are at least 28 weeks pregnant are advised to receive the vaccine as soon as possible, with vaccination continuing to be recommended for all eligible women throughout pregnancy up until delivery. Additionally, the vaccine will be reoffered for every subsequent pregnancy to maintain consistent coverage and protection. This monthly report includes vaccine uptake data for women who delivered in June 2026. Programme delivery The programme is delivered through GP and maternity services, ensuring accessible vaccination opportunities for eligible pregnant women across England. In addition, the RSV vaccine is available through the community pharmacy delivery model at approximately 37 sites across two ICBs in the East of England. It is also available at up to 200 additional community pharmacy sites across the Midlands, North West, and London commissioning regions during the 2025 to 2026 expansion phase. Previous monthly reports are available at RSV maternal vaccination coverage in England. View five news elements
Time
The report was published on 5 November 2026, and the data reflects vaccine uptake for women who delivered in June 2026, 22 months after the programme's launch in September 2024.
Location and population
The report focuses on England, specifically on NHS commissioning regions and integrated care boards (ICBs) within England, with data broken down by ethnic group.
Event
The report details the maternal RSV vaccination coverage in England, including uptake rates by NHS commissioning region, ICB, and ethnicity, as part of the national immunisation programme introduced in September 2024.
Scale, impact and risk
The RSV maternal vaccine uptake for June 2026 was 62.0%, with regional variations ranging up to 17.3 percentage points and ethnic variations up to 39.6 percentage points. The programme aims to reduce the burden of RSV-related illness, hospitalisations, and deaths, particularly among infants under six months and older adults.
Response, status and uncertainty
The report is an official statistics release, and the data is based on information from more than 97.5% of GP practices since January 2025. The data quality has improved, allowing for a more representative analysis of vaccine uptake across England.

Illness Levels Over Time | Respiratory Illnesses - Centers for Disease Control and Prevention | CDC (.gov)

News Brief
The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) published a report on November 6, 2026, focusing on respiratory illnesses, including respiratory syncytial virus (RSV). The report outlines the general public health significance of RSV, emphasizing its potential to cause severe respiratory infections in vulnerable groups such as infants and the elderly. However, no specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers are provided in the evidence. The CDC highlights the importance of monitoring RSV and its associated health risks, but does not detail any current outbreaks, investigations, or targeted responses. The report serves as a general informational resource rather than a specific outbreak update.

National flu and COVID-19 surveillance report: 5 November 2026 (week 45) - GOV.UK

National flu and COVID-19 surveillance report: 5 November 2026 (week 45) - GOV.UK
新闻简报The National Flu and COVID-19 Surveillance Report for 5 November 2026 (week 45) provides an overview of respiratory virus activity in England. The report is based on data up to week 44 (26 October to 1 November 2026) and highlights that respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) activity increased, circulating at baseline levels. Emergency department attendances for acute bronchiolitis rose but remained within seasonally expected ranges. The report also notes that influenza activity showed variable trends, with ED attendances for influenza-like illness slightly above seasonally expected levels. Influenza A positivity increased, while influenza B remained stable. The report acknowledges data quality issues, particularly in vaccine uptake reporting, and mentions that some pharmacy vaccinations may be misclassified. It does not provide specific case counts or risk levels for RSV, focusing instead on surveillance trends and methodological considerations.

规模、影响与风险

A high priorityPublic-health news

National flu and COVID-19 surveillance report: 5 November 2026 (week 45) - GOV.UK

News Brief
The National Flu and COVID-19 Surveillance Report for 5 November 2026 (week 45) provides an overview of respiratory virus activity in England. The report is based on data up to week 44 (26 October to 1 November 2026) and highlights that respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) activity increased, circulating at baseline levels. Emergency department attendances for acute bronchiolitis rose but remained within seasonally expected ranges. The report also notes that influenza activity showed variable trends, with ED attendances for influenza-like illness slightly above seasonally expected levels. Influenza A positivity increased, while influenza B remained stable. The report acknowledges data quality issues, particularly in vaccine uptake reporting, and mentions that some pharmacy vaccinations may be misclassified. It does not provide specific case counts or risk levels for RSV, focusing instead on surveillance trends and methodological considerations.
Original source text (English)
This publication is licensed under the terms of the Open Government Licence v3.0 except where otherwise stated. To view this licence, visit nationalarchives.gov.uk/doc/open-government-licence/version/3 or write to the Information Policy Team, The National Archives, Kew, London TW9 4DU, or email: psi@nationalarchives.gov.uk . Where we have identified any third party copyright information you will need to obtain permission from the copyright holders concerned. This publication is available at https://www.gov.uk/government/statistics/national-flu-and-covid-19-surveillance-reports-2026-to-2027-season/national-flu-and-covid-19-surveillance-report-5-november-2026-week-45 This report summarises the information from the surveillance systems which are used to monitor COVID-19 (caused by severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2)), influenza, respiratory syncytial virus ( RSV ) and diseases caused by other seasonal respiratory viruses in England. The report is based on data up to week 44 of 2026 (26 October to 1 November 2026). The main messages of this report are: Annual reports for the 2025 to 2026 season: Influenza in the UK, annual epidemiological report: winter 2025 to 2026 (published 28 May 2026) Epidemiology of COVID-19 in England (published 25 June 2026) Seasonal influenza vaccine uptake in all GP patients: annual report 2025 to 2026 (published 28 May 2026) Seasonal influenza vaccine uptake in school-aged children: annual report 2025 to 2026 (published 28 May 2026) Seasonal influenza vaccine uptake in frontline healthcare workers: annual report 2025 to 2026 (published 28 May 2026) Influenza activity showed variable trends across surveillance indicators and is circulating at low levels. Emergency department ( ED ) attendances for influenza-like-illness ( ILI ) increased slightly and were above seasonally expected levels. The number of influenza-confirmed acute respiratory infection ( ARI ) incidents decreased compared with the previous week. A briefing note has been issued to inform the NHS that influenza activity is now above baseline levels. Influenza vaccination data was unavailable or incomplete at the time of analysis in weeks 41 and 42 and led to several cohorts not being published in week 42. This has been corrected since week 43, and all cohorts are included. This will be corrected retrospectively in the report for week 42. There remains an issue that affects the attribution of vaccinations by provider setting. The correct identification of vaccinations administered in pharmacies cannot currently be reliably distinguished from those administered in general practices or other healthcare settings. As a result, some pharmacy vaccinations are likely to be misclassified as general practice or other healthcare settings, artificially increasing vaccination activity reported for general practices and other healthcare settings and reducing activity reported for pharmacies. This is not expected to affect overall vaccination uptake estimates, which are considered to remain an accurate reflection of the influenza vaccination programme. Compared with the equivalent week last season (week 44 data, 2025 to 2026), vaccine uptake is lower among patients aged 65 years and over (59.1% vs 61.5%), patients aged 6 months to under 65 years in clinical risk groups (27.9% vs 28.9%), those aged 2 years (33.3% vs 34.2%), and those aged 3 years (33.1% vs 34.3%). In contrast, vaccine uptake is higher among pregnant women (31.1% vs 29.7%). COVID-19 activity decreased and is circulating at low levels. ED attendances for COVID-19-like illness remained stable. The number of COVID-19 confirmed acute respiratory infections ( ARI ) incidents decreased compared with the previous week. By the end of week 44 2026, 47.7% of all people aged 75 years and over in England had been vaccinated with an autumn 2026 dose. Similarly, 20.5% of all people aged under 75 years with a weakened immune system in England had been vaccinated with an autumn 2026 dose since 1 October 2026. RSV activity increased and is circulating at baseline levels. ED attendances for acute bronchiolitis increased and were within seasonally expected levels. Note 1: these indicators use the moving epidemic method ( MEM ) and the mean standard deviation method ( MSD ) to define thresholds to determine their respective levels of activity. Further information on these methods can be found in Influenza surveillance in Europe: establishing epidemic thresholds by the Moving Epidemic Method and Setting thresholds to determine COVID-19 activity levels using the mean standard deviation ( MSD ) method, England, 2022 to 2024 . The MEM approach is well-established for some influenza surveillance indicators, however, for other indicators both the MEM and MSD are experimental and may be subject to future revision. Influenza laboratory surveillance and GP swabbing positivity have transitioned from using MEM to using MSD . These approaches will be considered alongside expert opinion and triangulation of other data sources. The Second Generation Surveillance System ( SGSS ) captures test result information for notifiable infectious diseases, including COVID-19 and influenza, from laboratories in England. The unified sample dataset ( USD ) stores all SARS-CoV-2 test results reported to SGSS , Respiratory DataMart and UKHSA laboratories, and is used to calculate the percentage of tests positive for SARS-CoV-2 among all SARS-CoV-2 tests conducted. As of 3 November 2026, there were a total of 1,730 COVID-19 cases identified in hospital settings in week 44, decreasing from 2,052 cases in the previous week. SARS-CoV-2 (COVID-19) PCR positivity in hospital settings decreased slightly in week 44, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 7.6% up to Sunday 1 November 2026 compared with 8.1% for the same period on the previous Sunday. Positivity rates were highest among those aged 85 years and over with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 11.1% up to Sunday 1 November 2026. This has remained stable compared to 10.8% in the same age group on the previous Sunday. Note 2: data from previous seasons is aligned by day. Note 3: testing policy and practice may change over time which can impact positivity rates, therefore comparisons over time should be interpreted with caution. Notable changes in testing policy occurred during 2022 to 2023, which are outlined in the data quality report . Note 4: the highlighted line corresponds to the age group in the subplot title, grey lines correspond to all other age groups. Influenza positivity increased in week 44, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 7.6% up to Sunday 1 November 2026. This is compared with 6.8% on the previous Sunday. By type, influenza A positivity increased in week 44, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 6.8% up to Sunday 1 November 2026. Influenza B positivity remained stable in the same week, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 0.3% up to Sunday 1 November 2026. Influenza A positivity rates were highest among those aged between 5 and 11 years, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 10.7% up to Sunday 1 November 2026. This has decreased from 15.8% in the same age group on the previous Sunday. Influenza B positivity rates for the same period were highest among those aged between 5 and 11 years, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 1.0%. This has decreased from 2.0% in the same age group on the previous Sunday. RSV swab-positivity based PCR test results reported through SGSS have been included as a pilot indicator from week 49 2025 onwards. RSV positivity increased in week 44, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 2.5% up to Sunday 1 November 2026, compared with 1.6% up to the previous Sunday. RSV positivity rates were highest in those aged between 0 and 4 years with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 16.0% up to Sunday 1 November 2026 in week 44. This has increased from 10.0% in the same age group on the previous Sunday. Respiratory DataMart is a sentinel laboratory-based surveillance system where participating laboratories report positive and negative test results for a number of respiratory viruses from samples primarily taken in hospital. A small proportion of primary care samples are also included in this reporting. In week 44, data is based on reporting from 9 out of the 14 sentinel laboratories. In week 44, 4,137 respiratory specimens reported through the Respiratory DataMart System were tested for influenza. There were 307 positive samples for influenza: 221 influenza A (not subtyped), 7 influenza A (H3N2), 72 influenza A (H1N1)pdm09, and 11 influenza B. Overall, influenza positivity increased slightly to 7.4% in week 44 compared with 6.4% in the previous week. In week 44, 4,918 respiratory specimens reported through the Respiratory DataMart System were tested for SARS-CoV-2. There were 367 positive samples for SARS-CoV-2. SARS-CoV-2 positivity remained stable at 7.5% compared with 7.7% in the previous week, with the highest positivity in those aged 80 years and over at 9.9%. RSV positivity increased to 2.9%, with the highest positivity in those aged under 5 years at 17.5%. Adenovirus positivity increased slightly to 1.6%, with the highest positivity in those aged under 5 years at 8.4%. Human metapneumovirus ( hMPV ) positivity increased to 0.7%, with the highest positivity in those aged under 5 years at 2.0%. Parainfluenza positivity increased to 3.6%, with the highest positivity in those aged between 5 and 14 years at 13.5%. Rhinovirus positivity decreased slightly to 14.6%, with the highest positivity in those aged under 5 years at 31.6%. DataMart data is provisional and subject to retrospective updates. Note 5: shading represents 95% confidence intervals. UKHSA conducts genomic surveillance of SARS-CoV-2 lineages. This section provides an overview of circulating lineages in England, derived from data on sequenced PCR -positive SARS-CoV-2 samples in SGSS . The prevalence of UKHSA -designated lineages among sequenced cases is presented in Figure 13. To account for reporting delays, we report the proportion of lineages within COVID-19 cases that have had a sequenced positive sample between 28 September 2026 and 11 October 2026. Note that low sequencing numbers, especially within the latest reporting period, will impact the accuracy of the prevalence estimates. These most recent figures should therefore be interpreted with caution. Note that lineages will be grouped independently from their parent lineage once they reach sufficient prevalence, and may be re-grouped into their parent lineage if their prevalence subsequently falls. The data quality report contains more information on lineage groupings. Analysis of viruses from primary and secondary care shows that circulating A(H1N1)pdm09 viruses show low genetic diversity and all belong to clade D.3.1.1. Observations published from the WHO influenza vaccine composition meeting (VCM) in September 2026 suggest low reactivity of some viruses within the D.3.1.1 subclade with post-infection ferret antisera raised against A/Missouri/11/2025 (H1N1)pdm09-like virus – the A(H1N1)pdm09 component in 2026 Southern and 2026/2027 Northern Hemisphere cell- and egg-based influenza vaccines - and human sera post-vaccination with the Southern Hemisphere vaccine 2026. It is not yet clear how vaccine effectiveness against clinical disease may be affected by these observations. Vaccines typically still provide protection against severe clinical disease when drifted strains are seen with only a modest reduction in effectiveness. The characterisation of circulating A(H3N2) viruses shows that A(H3N2) viruses are currently showing low genetic diversity. All sequenced viruses to date belong to genetic subclade K. To date, only a small number of influenza B viruses have been detected, which show some genetic diversity. Between week 35 2026 (week ending 30 August 2026) and week 44 2026 (week ending 1 November 2026), the UKHSA respiratory virus unit ( RVU ) has genetically characterised 351 seasonal influenza viruses, and identified 229 influenza A(H1N1)pdm09 viruses, 90 influenza A(H3N2) viruses, and 32 influenza B viruses. Details of the characterised viruses by subtype are shown in Tables 1, 2 and 3. The RVU has confirmed by genome sequencing the detection of live attenuated influenza vaccine ( LAIV ) viruses in 14 influenza A/B positive samples, collected from children aged between 2 and 16 years. UKHSA RVU performs antigenic characterisation of influenza A(H1N1)pdm09, influenza A(H3N2) and influenza B viruses using haemagglutination inhibition ( HI ) assays. Data from these assays are used to compare how similar the currently circulating influenza viruses are to the strains included in seasonal influenza vaccines, and to monitor for changes in circulating influenza viruses. Similarity of currently circulating influenza strains to vaccine strains is defined as having an antibody titre within 4-fold when compared with reference viruses representative of the vaccine strain. Antigenic characterisation of viruses in the current reporting period is underway. A(H1N1)pdm09: 5 A(H1N1)pdm09 viruses have been antigenically characterised and 3 were similar to reference viruses representative of the A/Missouri/11/2025 (H1N1)pdm09-like Northern Hemisphere 2026/27 (H1N1)pdm09 vaccine strains. A(H3N2): 6 A(H3N2) viruses have been antigenically characterised and 6 were similar to reference viruses representative of the A/Darwin/1454/2025 (H3N2)-like and A/Darwin/1415/2025 (H3N2)-like Northern Hemisphere 2026/27 (H3N2) vaccine strains. Influenza positive samples are screened for mutations in the virus neuraminidase ( NA ) and the cap-dependent endonuclease of the polymerase acidic protein (PA) genes known to confer neuraminidase inhibitor (oseltamivir and zanamivir) or baloxavir resistance, respectively. Results from this surveillance are given in table 4. There have been six detections with mutations related to reduced susceptibility/resistance to oseltamivir and/or reduced susceptibility to baloxavir. Data is presented on viral ARI incidents in different settings that are reported to UKHSA health protection teams ( HPTs ). Please note that reporting practices are known to vary between seasons and between regions. Any interpretation of temporal and regional trends should consider the likelihood of differences in reporting of ARI incidents over time and between regions. There were 103 new ARI incidents reported in week 44 in England. These included: 95 incidents from care homes, of which 16 were due to influenza A, 12 were due to SARS-CoV-2, 11 were due to other pathogens, 4 were due to multiple pathogens and 1 was due to influenza (no type information available). No pathogen was reported in 51 incidents 1 incident from hospitals, of which 1 was due to multiple pathogens 3 incidents from educational settings, of which 3 were due to RSV 4 incidents from other settings, of which 2 were due to SARS-CoV-2. No pathogen was reported in 2 incidents Syndromic surveillance collects data from various healthcare sources where presentations are classified by patterns of symptoms compatible with specific infections. In some settings, the syndromic diagnosis can be supplemented by (rapid) testing. In this report, ED attendances are displayed. Further details and data from other syndromic surveillance systems can be found in the syndromic surveillance weekly summaries . During the week ending on 01 November 2026, ED attendances for acute respiratory infections remained stable overall, however there were increases in the under 1 year age group. ED attendances for COVID-19-like illness remained stable. ED attendance for influenza-like illness increased slightly and were above seasonally expected levels; the increase was in adults aged 45 to 64 years. ED attendance for acute bronchiolitis (a syndrome related to RSV infection) increased and were within seasonally expected levels. Daily NHS 111 acute respiratory infection triaged calls remained stable. GP in-hours consultation rates for upper respiratory tract infections remained stable overall and at seasonally expected levels; however there were increases observed in the under 1 year age group. Consultations for bronchiolitis increased in the under 1 year age group while COVID-19-like and influenza-like illness consultations both decreased. GP out-of-hours daily contacts for acute respiratory infections were stable. Note 6: 7-day moving average is adjusted for bank holidays. Grey columns show weekends and bank holidays. Note 7: the coloured line is the 7-day moving average by age group. Grey lines show the other age groups. The y-axis shows daily attendances. It is adjusted for bank holidays. Primary care surveillance is undertaken in collaboration with the Royal College of General Practitioners ( RCGP ) Research and Surveillance Centre ( RSC ), using a national sentinel surveillance system of around 2,000 GP practices covering over 20 million registered patients of all ages across England. More information on the methodology can be found in the data quality report . The weekly ILI consultation rate through the RCGP surveillance decreased slightly to 6.2 per 100,000 registered population in participating GP practices in week 44 compared with 6.5 per 100,000 in the previous week. This rate is in the baseline activity level (Figure 20). By age group, the highest rates were seen in those aged under 1 year (12.0 per 100,000), and those aged between 1 and 4 years (8.8 per 100,000). The lower respiratory tract infections ( LRTI ) consultation rate remained stable at 84.1 per 100,000 in week 44 compared with 81.6 per 100,000 in the previous week. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

呼吸道合胞病毒 - 大英百科全书

respiratory syncytial virus - Britannica
新闻简报The article from Britannica provides an overview of respiratory syncytial virus (RSV), highlighting its role in causing mild cold-like illnesses and severe lower respiratory diseases, particularly in infants under six months and the elderly. It notes that RSV is seasonal, with annual epidemics during winter in temperate regions. The article states that an estimated 60 to 70 percent of children are infected with RSV by age one, and nearly all by age two. It also mentions that repeat infections are common due to the virus's ability to evade immunity. Prevention methods include frequent handwashing, cleaning surfaces, and avoiding close contact. Treatment options include the nucleoside analogue ribavirin, while a monoclonal antibody is available for prevention in high-risk infants and children. respiratory syncytial virus - Britannica

The article from Britannica provides an overview of respiratory syncytial virus (RSV), highlighting its role in causing mild cold-like illnesses and severe lower respiratory diseases, particularly in infants under six months and the elderly. It notes that RSV is seasonal, with annual epidemics during winter in temperate regions. She joined Britannica in 2006 and... Kara Rogers Fact-checked by Britannica Editors Encyclopaedia Britannica's editors oversee subject areas in which they have extensive knowledge, whether from years of experience gained by working on that content or via study for an advanced degree.... Britannica Editors Last updated Nov. 8, 2026 •History Britannica AI Ask Anything Table of Contents Table of Contents Ask Anything News • Medical societies unify to endorse evidence-based respiratory vaccine guidance • Nov. 5, 2026, 2:39 PM ET (News-Medical) ...(Show more) Cranking up a natural defense pathway boosts cells' resistance to RSV • Oct. 20, 2026, 12:40 PM ET (Medical Xpress) Show less respiratory syncytial virus (RSV), infectious agent of the respiratory system that typically produces a mild coldlike illness but in susceptible individuals, particularly infants under six months of age, is a major cause of severe and potentially fatal lower respiratory disease. Severe bronchiolitis and pneumonia caused by respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) occurs most commonly in the elderly and in young children. An estimated 60 to 70 percent of children have been infected with RSV by age one, and nearly all children have been infected by age two. Because RSV effectively evades immunity, repeat infections are common. RSV infection is seasonal, occurring as an annual epidemic during the winter in most countries in temperate regions. What's the Difference Between Bacteria and Viruses?Knowing the difference isn't just a technical distinction—it can save lives.(more) See all videos for this article Direct person-to-person contact is thought to be the most common route of RSV transmission. The virus also can be transmitted through the air, such as by coughing or sneezing, and through direct contact with a contaminated surface followed by touching of the face. Symptoms generally appear within four to six days of infection and include runny nose, cough, sore throat, headache, fever, and wheezing. In instances of severe illness, affected individuals may have a high fever, severe wheezing, rapid or laboured breathing, and bluish lips or fingers. Infants may only show signs of irritability, fatigue, and breathing difficulties, even in severe cases. The only pharmacological therapy available for treatment of RSV is the nucleoside analogue ribavirin, which can be administered orally, parenterally (by injection or infusion), or by inhalation. An injectable humanized monoclonal antibody is available for prevention of severe RSV illness in high-risk infants and children. It provides passive immunity and must by given by intramuscular injection once a month during RSV season. Transmission can be prevented with frequent handwashing, cleaning of surfaces, avoiding close contact with others, and covering the mouth and nose with the upper arm when coughing or sneezing. Respiratory Syncytial Virus Market Anticipated to Witness - openPR.com
新闻简报The report highlights the anticipated growth of the Respiratory Syncytial Virus (RSV) market by 2034, citing industry analysis and key players such as GSK, MedImmune, and Moderna. In October 2026, Enanta Pharmaceuticals initiated a Phase IIb/III trial for zelicapavir in adults at high risk of severe RSV outcomes, while Moderna's mRNA-1345 vaccine was evaluated in a Phase II trial for children aged 2 to under 18 years. The trial showed a favorable safety profile and strong neutralizing antibody responses against both RSV-A and RSV-B. In September 2026, Merck submitted a sBLA to the FDA for Enflonsia, aiming to expand its use to prevent RSV-associated lower respiratory tract disease in children under 2 years. The EMA also validated an application for this extension in the European Union. In 2023, the 7MM reported approximately 8.6 million RSV cases, with the United States contributing 56% of these. Children under five years old accounted for nearly 50% of the total cases. The report emphasizes the development of vaccines and therapies for RSV, including GSK's AREXVY, which was recommended for adults aged 50-59 in 2025. These developments suggest a growing focus on RSV prevention and treatment, particularly in high-risk populations.

The trial assessed the vaccine's safety, reactogenicity, and immunogenicity in healthy children and those at increased risk of RSV infection. Among the 346 vaccinated participants, mRNA-1345 demonstrated a favorable safety profile and elicited strong neutralizing antibody responses against both RSV-A and RSV-B. No vaccine-related serious adverse events or treatment discontinuations were reported. This group includes individuals with underlying health conditions such as COPD, asthma, diabetes, heart disease, and those living in care facilities. This recommendation builds on ACIP's earlier June 2024 vote endorsing RSV vaccination for adults aged 60-74 at increased risk, and all adults aged 75 and above. Respiratory Syncytial Virus Overview Respiratory Syncytial Virus (RSV) is a highly contagious virus that affects the respiratory system, primarily targeting the lungs and breathing passages. It commonly causes infections in the nose, throat, windpipe, and lungs. RSV is a significant cause of respiratory illness in infants and young children, often leading to mild cold-like symptoms such as coughing, sneezing, runny nose, fever, and sometimes wheezing. In older children and adults, RSV infections usually cause mild cold symptoms, but it can result in more severe respiratory problems in individuals with weakened immune systems or underlying health conditions.
